======================================================================
ARTICLE: Social media marketing
WIKIPEDIA REVISION TEXT
======================================================================

{{Short description|Promotion of products or services on social media}}
{{protection padlock|small=yes}}
{{multiple issues|
{{Update|date=April 2022}}
{{More citations needed|date=May 2023}}}}
{{Internet Marketing}}
[[File:(Fashion film) THYREN 2018 SPIRNG - SUMMER COLLECTION Fashion Ad Film (feat.Julia Ratner).webm|thumb|upright=0.8|thumbtime=25|A video advert for a fashion magazine]]

'''Social media marketing''' is the use of [[social media]] platforms and [[website]]s to promote a product or service.<ref>{{cite journal|last1=Felix|first1=R.|last2=Rauschnabel|first2=P.A. |last3=Hinsch|first3=C. |title= Elements of Strategic Social Media Marketing: A Holistic Framework|journal= Journal of Business Research |year= 2016|volume=70|pages=118–126 |doi=10.1016/j.jbusres.2016.05.001}}</ref> Although the terms [[e-marketing]] and [[digital marketing]] are still dominant in academia, social media marketing is becoming more popular for practitioners and researchers.<ref>{{Cite journal|last=Shaltoni|first=AM |date=2016-07-01|title=E-marketing education in transition: An analysis of international courses and programs|journal=The International Journal of Management Education|language=en|volume=14|issue=2|pages=212–218|doi=10.1016/j.ijme.2016.04.004|issn=1472-8117}}</ref>

Social media platforms such as [[Facebook]], [[LinkedIn]], [[Instagram]], and [[X (social network)|X]], among others, have built-in data [[analytics]] tools that companies can use to track the progress, success, and engagement of social media marketing campaigns. Companies address a range of [[Stakeholder (corporate)|stakeholders]] through social media marketing, including current and potential customers, current and potential employees, [[journalist]]s, [[blogger]]s, and the general public.

On a strategic level, social media marketing includes the management of a marketing campaign, [[governance]], setting the scope (e.g. more active or passive use) and the establishment of a firm's desired social media "culture" and "tone".

Firms that use social media marketing can allow customers and [[Internet]] users to post [[user-generated content]] (e.g., online comments, product reviews, etc.), also known as "[[earned media]]", rather than use marketer-prepared [[advertising]] copy.

==Purposes and tactics==
[[Social media]] may be employed in marketing as a communications tool that makes companies accessible to those who are interested in their product and visible to those who are not familiar with their products.<ref>{{cite journal|author=Neti, S.|url=http://www.ijecbs.com/July2011/13.pdf|title=Social Media and Its Role in Marketing|journal=International Journal of Enterprise Computing and Business Systems|year=2011|volume=1|issue=2|access-date=2014-06-06|archive-date=2015-04-04|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150404012807/http://www.ijecbs.com/July2011/13.pdf|url-status=live}}</ref> It is used by companies to create buzz, learn from customers, and target them.<ref>{{cite journal|author=Saravanakumar, M., Suganthalakshmi, T.|url=http://dems.unimib.it/corsi/817/esercitazioni/social_media_mktg.pdf|title=Social Media Marketing|journal=Life Science Journal|year=2012|volume=9|issue=4|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140607003438/http://dems.unimib.it/corsi/817/esercitazioni/social_media_mktg.pdf|archive-date=2014-06-07}}</ref>

Of the top 10 factors that correlate with a strong [[Google]] organic search, seven are social media-dependent. This means that if brands with little to no social media presence tend to show up less on Google searches.<ref name="Mahapatra">{{cite web|last1=Mahapatra|first1=Lisa|title=Social Media Marketing: How Do Top Brands Use Social Platforms?|url=http://www.ibtimes.com/social-media-marketing-how-do-top-brands-use-social-platforms-charts-1379457|website=IBTimes.com|publisher=International Business Times|access-date=10 June 2014|date=2013-08-09|archive-date=2014-06-03|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140603184028/http://www.ibtimes.com/social-media-marketing-how-do-top-brands-use-social-platforms-charts-1379457|url-status=live}}</ref> While platforms such as [[Twitter]], [[Facebook]] and{{--}}in the past{{--}}[[Google+]] have a larger number of monthly users, the visual media-sharing-based mobile platforms garner a higher interaction rate in comparison, and in 2013 registered the fastest growth, changing the ways in which consumers engage with brand content. In 2013, [[Instagram]] had an interaction rate of 1.46% with an average of 130 million users monthly as opposed to Twitter, which had a .03% interaction rate with an average of 210 million monthly users.<ref name="Mahapatra" /> Unlike traditional media that are often cost-prohibitive to many companies, a social media strategy does not require significant financial investment.<ref>{{cite journal|author=Hanna, R., Rohm, A., Crittenden V. |title=We're all connected: The power of the social media ecosystem|journal= Business Horizons |year= 2011|volume=54|issue= 3|doi=10.1016/j.bushor.2011.01.007|pages=265–273}}</ref>

To this end, companies make use of platforms such as Facebook, X (formerly Twitter), Pinterest, Instagram to reach audiences much wider than through traditional print, television, or radio advertisements alone at a fraction of the cost, as most social networking sites can be used at little or no cost (however, some websites charge companies for premium services). This has changed the ways that companies approach and interact with customers, as a substantial percentage of consumer interactions are now being carried out over online platforms with much higher visibility. Customers can post reviews of products and services, rate [[customer service]], and ask questions or voice concerns directly to companies through social media platforms.

According to ''Measuring Success'', over 80% of consumers use the web to research products and services.<ref>Funk T. (2013) Measuring Success. In: Advanced Social Media Marketing. Apress, Berkeley, CA.</ref> Thus social media marketing is also used by businesses in order to build relationships of trust with consumers.{{cn|date=June 2026}} To this aim, companies may hire personnel to specifically handle these social media interactions, who usually report under the title of [[online community manager]]s. Handling these interactions in a satisfactory manner can result in an increase of consumer trust. To both this aim and to fix the public's perception of a company, three steps are taken in order to address consumer concerns:{{cn|date=June 2026}}
* Identifying the extent of the social chatter
* Engaging the [[influencers]] to help
* Developing a proportional response

==Strategies==
===Passive approach===
Social media can be a useful source of market information and a way to hear customers' perspectives. Blogs, content communities, and forums are platforms where individuals share their reviews and recommendations of brands, products, and services. Businesses are able to tap into and analyze customer voices and feedback generated in social media for marketing purposes.<ref name="Constantinides E. 2008">{{cite book|author1= Constantinides E.|author2= Lorenzo C.|author3= Gómez M.A.|title= European Retail Research|chapter= Social Media: A New Frontier for Retailers?|year= 2008|volume= 22|issue= 1|pages= 1–28|doi= 10.1007/978-3-8349-8099-1_1|isbn= 978-3-8349-8099-1|url= https://research.utwente.nl/en/publications/social-media-a-new-frontier-for-retailers(2042de4c-cf4d-4f0f-a9d6-43ae725bf513).html|access-date= 2018-12-27|archive-date= 2022-08-14|archive-url= https://web.archive.org/web/20220814100028/https://research.utwente.nl/en/publications/social-media-a-new-frontier-for-retailers|url-status= live}}</ref> In this sense, social media is a relatively inexpensive source of [[market intelligence]] which can be used by marketers and managers to track and respond to consumer-identified problems and detect market opportunities.

===Active approach===
Social media can be used as a [[public relations]] tool, a [[direct marketing]] tool, and a communication channel to target very specific audiences, with [[social media influencers]] and social media personalities as effective [[customer engagement]] tools.<ref name="Constantinides E. 2008"/> This tactic is widely known as [[influencer marketing]], which gives brands the opportunity to reach their target audience via a group of selected [[Internet celebrity|influencers]] advertising their product or service. Brands were projected to spend up to $15 billion on influencer marketing by 2022, per ''[[Business Insider Intelligence]]'' estimates, based on Mediakix data.<ref>{{Cite web|url=https://www.businessinsider.com/influencer-marketing-report|title=Influencer Marketing: State of the social media influencer market in 2020|last=Schomer|first=Audrey|website=Business Insider|access-date=2020-03-18|archive-date=2021-08-05|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20210805081226/https://www.businessinsider.com/influencer-marketing-report|url-status=live}}</ref> The use of customer influencers, such as popular bloggers, can be an efficient and cost-effective method to launch new products or services.<ref>Gillin, P. (2007): The New Influencers, a Marketer's Guide to the new Social Media, Sanger, CA.</ref>

====Trend simulation====
'''Trend simulation''' is a [[digital marketing]] approach to [[Manufacturing Consent|manufacturing interest]], typically through [[social media marketing]] through the deployment of burner pages, [[shill|fake accounts]], and volume posting.<ref>{{cite news |last1=Kornhaber |first1=Spencer |title=Music's Next 'Disco Sucks' Moment Is Near |url=https://www.theatlantic.com/culture/2026/05/music-authenticity-chaotic-good-geese/687081/ |work=The Atlantic |date=7 May 2026 |language=en}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |last1=Robinson |first1=Kristin |title=Burner Pages and Volume Posting: Inside Music's Hottest New TikTok Marketing Strategy |url=https://www.billboard.com/pro/music-hottest-tiktok-marketing-strategy-burner-pages-volume/ |work=Billboard |date=20 August 2025}}</ref> The technique 's artificial seeding to steer discourse and induce [[viral marketing]] has been criticized as an unethical [[psyop]].<ref>{{cite news |last1=LaPierre |first1=Megan |title=Shocking: Popular Album Had a Marketing Campaign |url=https://exclaim.ca/music/article/shocking-popular-album-had-a-marketing-campaign |publisher=Exclaim |date=Apr 15, 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |last1=Soni |first1=Shreya |title=Data centers, AI influencers and Everlane: June 2026 cultural and consumer trends |url=https://www.foresightfactory.co/blog/june-2026-cultural-consumer-trends-data-centers-ai-influencers-everlane/ |work=Foresight Factory |date=3 June 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |last1=Semley |first1=John |title=The Fanfare Around the Band Geese Actually Was a Psyop |url=https://www.wired.com/story/geese-chaotic-good-marketing-industry-plant/ |work=Wired |date=14 April 2026}}</ref><ref>{{cite news |title=Fake TikTok fans and pop music's 'unethical' marketing |url=https://www.dw.com/en/tiktok-spotify-social-media-indie-music-unethical-marketing-pop-charts/a-77091563 |work=dw.com |language=en}}</ref>

==Engagement==
Engagement with the social web means that customers and stakeholders are active participants rather than passive spectators. An example of these are [[consumer advocacy group]]s and groups that criticize companies (e.g., [[lobby group]]s or [[advocacy organization]]s). The use of [[social media]] in a business or political context allows people to express and share opinions about a company's products, services or business practices, or a government's actions.

On social media, each participant becomes part of the marketing department (or a challenge to the marketing effort) as other customers read their comments or reviews. The effectiveness of social media marketing campaigns is dependent on the promotion of online engagement.<ref>{{cite book|author=Evans, Dave|title=Social Media Marketing: The Next Generation of Business Engagement|url=https://books.google.com/books?id=7l2OR6giC6AC&pg=PT15|access-date=28 July 2013|date=16 September 2010|publisher=John Wiley & Sons|isbn=978-0-470-94421-9|pages=15–|archive-date=8 January 2014|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140108020136/http://books.google.com/books?id=7l2OR6giC6AC&pg=PT15|url-status=live}}</ref> With the advent of social media marketing, it has become increasingly important to gain customer interest in products and services, which can eventually be translated into buying behavior, or voting and donating behavior in a political context. New online marketing concepts of engagement and loyalty have emerged which aim to build customer participation and brand reputation.<ref>{{cite book|title=Social Media Marketing|last=Evans|first=Dave|date=2012-01-01|publisher=Sybex|isbn=978-1-118-19449-2}}</ref>

Engagement in social media for the purpose of a social media strategy is divided into two parts. The first is proactive, regular posting of new online content, which can be seen through [[digital photo]]s, [[digital video]]s, text, and conversations. It is also represented through sharing of content and information from others via weblinks. The second part is reactive conversations, with social media users responding to those who reach out to others' social media profiles through comments or messages.<ref>Schaffer, Neal. Maximize Your Social : A One-Stop Guide to Building a Social Media Strategy for Marketing and Business Success. Somerset, NJ, USA: John Wiley & Sons, 2013. ProQuest ebrary. Web. 3 December 2014.
Copyright © 2013. John Wiley & Sons. All rights reserved.</ref>

==Campaigns==
[[File:Business use of social media in the UK.png|thumb|Business use of social media in the UK in 2012]]

===Local businesses===
Small businesses use social networking sites as a promotional technique. Businesses can follow individuals' social media usage in their local area and advertise specials and deals, which can be exclusive and in the form of "get a free drink with a copy of this tweet". This type of message encourages other locals to follow the business on their official websites in order to obtain the promotional deal. The business's brand visibility is enhanced in the process.

Social networking sites are also used by small businesses to develop their own [[market research]] on new products and services. By encouraging their customers to give feedback on new product ideas, businesses can gain insights on whether or not a product may be accepted by their target market enough to merit full production. In addition, customers will feel the company has engaged them in the process of co-creation—the process in which the business uses customer feedback to create or modify a product or service to fill a need of the target market. Such feedback can be presented in various forms, such as surveys, contests, and polls.

Social networking sites such as LinkedIn, also provide opportunities for small businesses to find candidates to fill staff positions.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.adweek.com/socialtimes/survey-96-of-recruiters-use-social-media-to-find-high-quality-candidates/627040|title=Survey: 92% of Recruiters Use Social Media to Find High-Quality Candidates|date=22 September 2015 |access-date=6 March 2018|archive-date=19 September 2018|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180919081138/https://www.adweek.com/socialtimes/survey-96-of-recruiters-use-social-media-to-find-high-quality-candidates/627040|url-status=live}}</ref> Review sites such as [[Yelp]] help small businesses build their reputation beyond brand visibility. Positive customer peer reviews help influence new prospects to purchase goods and services more than company advertising.<ref>{{cite web|last1=Leonard |first1=Jay |url=http://www.business2community.com/infographics/impact-online-reviews-customers-buying-decisions-infographic-01280945#WHA7GjedRY1ami50.97|title=The Impact of Online Reviews on Customers' Buying Decisions [Infographic]|work=Business2Community |date=26 July 2015|access-date=6 March 2018|archive-date=14 May 2018|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180514150601/https://www.business2community.com/infographics/impact-online-reviews-customers-buying-decisions-infographic-01280945#WHA7GjedRY1ami50.97|url-status=live}}</ref>

== Benefits ==
Social media marketing allows companies to promote themselves to large, diverse audiences that could not be reached through traditional marketing such as phone and email-based advertising. Marketing on most social media platforms also comes at little to no cost, making it accessible to virtually any size business. Social media marketing accommodates personalized and direct marketing that targets specific demographics and markets. Companies can engage with customers directly, allowing them to obtain feedback and resolve issues almost immediately.<ref name="Ramsaran-Fowdar-2013">{{Cite journal |last=Ramsaran-Fowdar |first=Rooma Roshnee |date=2013-03-31 |title=The Implications of Facebook Marketing for Organizations |url=http://www.cmr-journal.org/article/view/9710 |url-status=live |journal=Contemporary Management Research |volume=9 |issue=1 |pages=73–84 |doi=10.7903/cmr.9710 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20200406084240/http://www.cmr-journal.org/article/view/9710 |archive-date=6 April 2020 |access-date=19 April 2020 |doi-access=free}}</ref>

Social media marketing is an ideal environment for a company to conduct market research.<ref name="Parsons-2018">{{Cite journal |last1=Parsons |first1=Amy L. |last2=Lepkowska-White |first2=Elzbieta |date=2018-04-03 |title=Social Media Marketing Management: A Conceptual Framework |journal=Journal of Internet Commerce |language=en |volume=17 |issue=2 |pages=81–95 |doi=10.1080/15332861.2018.1433910 |issn=1533-2861 |s2cid=158177839}}</ref> It can be used as a means of obtaining information about competitors and boost competitive advantage. Social platforms can be used to promote brand events, deals, and news. Social platforms can also be used to offer incentives in the form of loyalty points and discounts. It allows companies to build an online platform to promote and sell their product.<ref name="Parsons-2018" />

Social media marketing can also be useful for gaining customers who would not know about the business otherwise.<ref>{{cite encyclopedia |last=Davies |first=E |date=2018 |encyclopedia= |title=Getting Started with Social Media Marketing }}{{full citation needed|date=September 2026}}</ref> It builds a community that enhances a business's reach in their selected target market.<ref>{{Cite journal |last1=Flanigan |first1=Rod L. |last2=Obermier |first2=Timothy R. |date=2016 |title=An Assessment of the Use of Social Media in the Industrial Distribution Business-to-Business Market Sector |journal=The Journal of Technology Studies |volume=42 |issue=1 |pages=18–29 |doi=10.21061/jots.v42i1.a.2 |jstor=90018734 |issn=1071-6084|doi-access=free }}</ref>

=== Advertising campaigns ===
To promote the 2013 film ''[[Monsters University]]'', Disney/Pixar created a Tumblr account, MUGrumblr, saying that the account is maintained by a 'Monstropolis transplant' and 'self-diagnosed coffee addict' who is currently a sophomore at Monsters University.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.stitchkingdom.com/disney-monsters-university-grumblr-viral-site-live-61559/|title='Monsters University' Grumblr Viral Site Goes Live – Stitch Kingdom|work=Stitch Kingdom|date=18 March 2013 |url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130321065137/http://www.stitchkingdom.com/disney-monsters-university-grumblr-viral-site-live-61559/|archive-date=2013-03-21|access-date=12 September 2015}}</ref> A "student" from Monsters University uploaded memes, animated GIFs, and Instagram-like photos related to the movie.

In 2014, Apple created a Tumblr page to promote the [[iPhone 5c]], labeling it "Every color has a story" with the website name "ISee5c". The site's main page is covered with different colors representing the iPhone 5c phone colors and case colors. When a colored section is clicked, a 15-second video plays a song and "showcases the dots featured on the rear of the iPhone 5c official cases and on the iOS 7 dynamic wallpapers",<ref>{{cite web|last1=Gurman |first1=Mark |url=http://9to5mac.com/2014/03/02/apple-seemingly-turns-to-tumblr-to-promote-the-iphone-5c/|title=Apple seemingly turns to Tumblr to promote the iPhone 5c|work=9to5Mac|date=2014-03-02|access-date=2014-06-11|archive-date=2014-06-14|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140614021151/http://9to5mac.com/2014/03/02/apple-seemingly-turns-to-tumblr-to-promote-the-iphone-5c/|url-status=live}}</ref> concluding with words that are related to the video's theme.

==Marketing techniques==
Social media marketing involves the use of [[social network]]s, ''[[COBRA (consumer theory)|consumer's online brand-related activities]]'' (COBRA) and electronic word of mouth to advertise online.<ref name="muntinga" /><ref name="Schivinski 64–80">{{Cite journal|last1=Schivinski|first1=Bruno|last2=Christodoulides|first2=George|last3=Dabrowski|first3=Dariusz|date=2016-03-01|title=Measuring Consumers' Engagement With Brand-Related Social-Media Content|journal=Journal of Advertising Research|language=en|volume=56|issue=1|pages=64–80|doi=10.2501/JAR-2016-004|s2cid=167329509|issn=0021-8499}}</ref> Social networks such as [[Facebook]] and [[Twitter]] provide advertisers with information about the likes and dislikes of their consumers.<ref name="kincy"/> This technique provides businesses with target audiences.<ref name="kincy"/>

With social networks, information relevant to users' likes is available to businesses, who then advertise accordingly. Uploading pictures of one's own recently purchased products is an example of a ''consumer's online brand-related activity''.<ref name="muntinga">{{cite journal|last=Muntinga|first=Daniel|author2=Moorman, M. |author3=Smit, E. |title=Introducing COBRAs exploring motivations for brand-related social media use|journal=International Journal of Advertising|year=2011|volume=30|issue=1|pages=13–46|doi=10.2501/IJA-30-1-013-046|s2cid=82881875}}</ref><ref name="Schivinski 64–80" /> Electronic recommendations and appraisals are a convenient manner to have a product promoted via consumer-to-consumer interactions.<ref name="muntinga" /> An example of electronic word of mouth would be an online hotel review;<ref name="kim">{{cite journal|last=Kim|first=Ellen|author2=Mattila, A. |author3=Baloglu, S. |title=Effects of gender and expertise on consumers' motivation to read online hotel reviews|journal=Cornell Hospitality Quarterly|year=2011|volume=52|issue=4|pages=399–406|doi=10.1177/1938965510394357|s2cid=154467192}}</ref> the hotel company can have two possible outcomes based on their service. A good service would result in a positive review, which gets the hotel free advertising via [[social media]]. However, a poor service will result in a negative consumer review, which can potentially harm the company's reputation.<ref>{{Cite journal|last1=Schivinski|first1=Bruno|last2=Muntinga|first2=Daan G.|last3=Pontes|first3=Halley M.|last4=Lukasik|first4=Przemyslaw|date=2019-02-10|title=Influencing COBRAs: the effects of brand equity on the consumer's propensity to engage with brand-related content on social media|journal=Journal of Strategic Marketing|volume=29|language=en|pages=1–23|doi=10.1080/0965254X.2019.1572641|s2cid=169721474|issn=0965-254X|url=https://eprints.bbk.ac.uk/26176/3/26176.pdf|access-date=2020-08-27|archive-date=2022-08-14|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220814095921/https://eprints.bbk.ac.uk/id/eprint/26176/3/26176.pdf|url-status=live}}</ref>

Social networking sites such as Facebook, Instagram, and Twitter have all influenced the buzz of word-of-mouth marketing. In 1999, Misner said that word-of mouth marketing is, "the world's most effective, yet least understood marketing strategy".<ref>{{cite journal | last1 = Trusov | first1 = M. | last2 = Bucklin | first2 = R. E. | last3 = Pauwels | first3 = K. | year = 2009 | title = Effects of word-of-mouth versus traditional marketing: Findings from an internet social networking site | journal = Journal of Marketing | volume = 73 | issue = 5| pages = 90–102 | doi = 10.1509/jmkg.73.5.90| hdl = 10679/56 | s2cid = 12434180 | hdl-access = free }}</ref> Through the influence of [[opinion leader]]s, the increased online "buzz" of word-of-mouth marketing that products, services or companies experience is due to the rise in use of social media and [[smartphone]]s. Businesses and marketers have noticed that, "a person's behaviour is influenced by many small groups".<ref name="Kotler">Kotler, P., Burton, S., Deans, K., Brown, L., & Armstrong, G. (2013). marketing (9th ed.). NSW, Australia: Pearson Australia</ref>

These small groups rotate around social networking accounts that are run by influential people (opinion leaders or "thought leaders") who have followers of groups. The types of groups (followers) are called:<ref name="Kotler"/>
* Reference groups (people who either know each other face-to-face or indirectly influence other people's attitudes or behaviours)
* Membership groups (people who directly influence other people's attitudes or behaviours)
* Aspirational groups (groups which an individual wishes to belong to)

===Blogs===
[[File:Wp-twentyten.jpg|thumb|A Wordpress blog]]

Platforms like [[LinkedIn]] create an environment for companies and clients to connect online.<ref name=Deis>{{cite journal|last1=Deis|first1=Michael H.|first2=Kyle |last2=Hensel |title=Using social media to increase advertising and improve marketing|journal=Entrepreneurial Executive|year=2010|page=87}}</ref> Companies that recognize the need for information, originality and accessibility employ [[blog]]s to make their products popular and unique/ and ultimately reach out to consumers who are privy to social media.<ref name=Chiang>{{cite journal|last=Chiang|first=I-Ping|author2=Chung-Hsien Hsieh |title=Exploring the impact of blog marketing on consumers|journal=Social Behavior and Personality|date=October 2011|pages=1245–1250|doi=10.2224/sbp.2011.39.9.1245|volume=39|issue=9|doi-access=free}}</ref>

Studies from 2009 show that consumers view coverage in the media or from bloggers as being more neutral and credible than print advertisements, which are not thought of as free or independent.<ref name="Rinallo">{{cite journal|last1=Rinallo|first1=Diego|first2=Suman|last2=Basuroy|title=Does Advertising Spending Influence Media Coverage of the Advertiser?|journal=Journal of Marketing|date=November 2009|pages=33–46|volume=73|issue=6|doi=10.1509/jmkg.73.6.33|s2cid=27883769}}</ref> Blogs allow a product or company to provide longer descriptions of products or services, may include testimonials and links to related social media or blog content.

Blogs can be updated frequently and are promotional techniques for keeping [[customers]], and also for acquiring followers and subscribers who can then be directed to social network pages. Online communities can enable a business to reach the clients of other businesses using the platform. To allow firms to measure their standing in the corporate world, sites enable employees to place evaluations of their companies.<ref name="Deis" />

Some businesses opt out of integrating social media platforms into their traditional marketing regimen. There are also specific corporate standards that apply when interacting online.<ref name="Deis"/> To maintain an advantage in a business-consumer relationship, businesses have to be aware of four key assets that consumers maintain: information, involvement, community, and control.<ref name=Kerr>{{cite journal|last=Kerr|first=Gayle|title=Buy, boycott or blog|journal=European Journal of Marketing|date=January 2012|volume=46|pages=387–405|doi=10.1108/03090561211202521|url=http://eprints.qut.edu.au/53367/|issue=3/4|s2cid=166402603|display-authors=etal|access-date=2013-01-11|archive-date=2013-05-13|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20130513131652/http://eprints.qut.edu.au/53367/|url-status=live}}</ref>

=== Influencer marketing ===
{{main|Influencer marketing}}
[[File:Chiara Ferragni - Giorgio Armani Show - Milan Fashion Week - 23 Sept. 2013.jpg|thumb|[[Chiara Ferragni]] is a [[fashion influencer]] and blogger known for her sponsored fashion posts.]]
Marketers target [[influencer]]s on social media that are recognized as being opinion leaders and opinion-formers based on the credibility of their following. An influencer's role under a brand [[Sponsorships|sponsorship]] is to send messages to their target audiences through posts to amplify the credibility of a product or brand. A social media post by an opinion leader can have a much greater impact (via the forwarding or "liking" of the post) than a social media post by a regular user.

Influencers can help brands obtain more consumers by promoting their products in an honest and genuine way using personal sales methods, which is why brands consider collaborations with influencers to be a smart strategy. However, influencer marketing works well because it uses real, shareable, and viral content to reach a large audience and provide a profitable return on investment.

Marketers have realized that "consumers are more prone to believe in other individuals" who they trust.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Sepp |first1=M. |last2=Liljander |first2=V. |last3=Gummerus |first3=J. |year=2011 |title=Private bloggers' motivations to produce content – a gratifications theory perspective |journal=Journal of Marketing Management |volume=27 |issue=13/14 |pages=1479–1503 |doi=10.1080/0267257X.2011.624532 |s2cid=167661592}}</ref> Opinion leaders can also send their own messages about the products and services that they choose.<ref>Fill, C., Hughes, G., & De Francesco, S. (2013). Advertising Strategy, creativity and media. London, UK: Pearson.</ref> They have strong following bases because their opinions are valued or trusted.<ref>{{cite journal |last1=Clement |first1=M. |last2=Proppe |first2=D. |last3=Rott |first3=A. |year=2007 |title=Do critics make bestsellers? Opinion leaders and the success of books |journal=Media Economics |volume=20 |issue=2 |pages=77–105 |doi=10.1080/08997760701193720 |s2cid=144453659}}</ref> Because of their personality, beliefs, values, and other characteristics, they have the potential to influence other people.<ref name="Kotler" /> They usually have a large number of followers otherwise known as their reference, membership or aspirational group.<ref name="Kotler" />

An opinion leader's support of a product (by posting a photo, video or written recommendation on a blog) can influence their followers and increase the chance of the brand selling more products or creating a following base of its own.<ref name="Kotler, P. 2013">Kotler, P., Burton, S., Deans, K., Brown, L., & Armstrong, G. (2013). marketing (9th ed.). NSW, Australia: Pearson Australia.</ref> The adjusted communication model shows the use of using opinion leaders and opinion formers. The sender/source gives the message to many opinion leaders, who pass the message on along with their personal opinions. The receivers form their own opinions and send their personal messages to their friends and family.<ref>Dahlen, M., Lange, F., & Smith, T. (2010). Marketing communications: A brand narrative approach. West Sussex, UK: John Wiley & Sons.</ref>

=== Organic social media ===
In contrast with pre-Internet marketing, such as TV ads and newspaper ads, in which marketers controlled all aspects of their ads, social media users are free to post comments right below online ads or post by companies about their products.

Companies are increasing the use of their social media strategies as part of their traditional marketing efforts via [[magazine]]s, newspapers, radio advertisements, and television advertisements. Since the 2010s, media consumers have often used multiple platforms at the same time (e.g. surfing the Internet on a tablet while watching a streaming TV show), so consistency of marketing content across all platforms has become necessary.

Heath (2006) wrote about the extent of attention that businesses should give to their social media sites. It is about finding a balance between frequently posting, but not over-posting. There is a lot more attention to be paid towards social media sites because people need updates to gain [[brand recognition]]. Therefore, a lot more content is needed, and this can often be unplanned content.<ref>{{cite journal | last1 = Heath | first1 = R. | last2 = Brandt | first2 = D. | last3 = Narin | first3 = A. | year = 2006 | title = Brand relationships: Strengthened by emotion, weakened by attention | journal = Journal of Advertising Research | volume = 46 | issue = 4| pages = 410–419 | doi=10.2501/s002184990606048x| s2cid = 54530013 }}</ref>

Planned content begins with the creative/marketing team generating their ideas. Once they have completed their ideas, they send them off for approval. There are two general ways to do so:
* The first is where each sector approves the plan one after another, editor, brand, followed by the legal team.<ref name="quepublishing.com">Brito, M. (2013). How content governance will facilitate media company transformation. InYour brand (pp. 3-7). Retrieved from http://www.quepublishing.com/articles/article.aspx?p=2143149&seqNum=3 {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160416031528/http://www.quepublishing.com/articles/article.aspx?p=2143149&seqNum=3 |date=2016-04-16 }}</ref> Sectors may differ depending on the size and philosophy of the business. 
* The second is where each sector is given 24 hours (or such designated time) to sign off or disapprove. If no action is given within the 24-hour period, the original plan is implemented.

Planned content is often noticeable to customers and is un-original or lacks excitement, but is also a safer option to avoid unnecessary backlash from the public.<ref name="quepublishing.com"/> Both routes for planned content are time-consuming as in the above; on the first pathway to approval, content takes 72 hours to be approved. Although the second route can be significantly shorter, it also holds more risk, particularly in the legal department.

Unplanned content is an "in the moment" idea, "a spontaneous, tactical reaction".<ref>Cramer, 2014, p.&nbsp;6</ref> The content could be trending and not have the time to take the planned content route. The unplanned content is posted sporadically and is not calendar/date/time arranged (Deshpande, 2014).<ref>{{cite journal | last1 = Cramer | first1 = T | year = 2014 | title = The rise of content marketing | journal = EContent | volume = 37 | issue = 3| pages = 6–10 }}</ref><ref>Deshpande, P. (2014, August 24). The content marketing pyramid: Create more with less. Retrieved April 1, 2016, from http://contentmarketinginstitute.com {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140701042402/http://contentmarketinginstitute.com/ |date=2014-07-01 }}</ref> Issues with unplanned content revolve around legal issues and whether the message being sent out represents the business/brand accordingly. If a company sends out a Tweet or Facebook message too hurriedly, the company may unintentionally use insensitive language or messaging that could alienate some consumers.

For example, celebrity chef [[Paula Deen]] was criticized after she made a social media post commenting about [[HIV-AIDS]] and [[South Africa]]; her message was deemed offensive by many observers. The main difference between planned and unplanned is the time to approve the content. Unplanned content must still be approved by marketing managers, but in a much more rapid manner e.g. 1–2 hours or less. Sectors may miss errors because of being hurried.

When using unplanned content Brito (2013) says, "be prepared to be reactive and respond to issues when they arise".<ref name="quepublishing.com" /> Brito writes about having a, "crisis escalation plan", because, "It will happen". The plan involves breaking down the issue into topics and classifying the issue into groups. "[I]dentify[ing] and flag[ging] potential risks" also helps to organise an issue. The problem can then be handled by the correct team and dissolved more effectively rather than any person at hand trying to solve the situation.<ref name="quepublishing.com" />

==== Platforms ====
{{main|Social media}}

Individuals, businesses, and other organizations can interact with one another and build relationships and communities online through [[social networking websites]]. Consumers can directly interact with companies that join these social channels.<ref>Zhang, M., Jansen, B. J., and Chowdhury, A. (2011) [https://faculty.ist.psu.edu/jjansen/academic/jansen_business_twitter.pdf Influence of Business Engagement in Online Word-of-mouth Communication on Twitter: A Path Analysis] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20170809103336/https://faculty.ist.psu.edu/jjansen/academic/jansen_business_twitter.pdf |date=2017-08-09 }}. Electronic Markets: The International Journal on Networked Business. 21(3), 161-175.</ref> These interactions can be more personal to users than traditional methods of [[Marketing communications|outbound marketing]] and advertising.<ref name="Assaad">{{cite journal|last=Assaad|first=Waad|author2=Jorge Marx Gomez|title=Social Network in marketing (Social Media Marketing) Opportunities and Risks|volume=2|issue=1|url=https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=3471150|journal=International Journal of Managing Public Sector Information and Communication Technologies|date=2011 |ssrn=3471150 }}</ref> The ability to rapidly change buying patterns and product or service acquisition and activity to a growing number of consumers is defined as an influence network.<ref name="Change">{{cite journal|last1=Chang|first1=Ray|first2=Wonseok|last2=Oh|first3=Alain|last3=Pinsonneault|first4=Dowan |last4=Kwon |title=A Network Perspective of Digital Competition in Online Advertising Industries: A Simulation-Based Approach|journal=Information Systems Research|volume=21|issue=3|pages=571–593|jstor= 23015551|year=2010|doi=10.1287/isre.1100.0302}}</ref>

On social networking sites and [[blog]]s, users can repost comments made by others about a product being promoted, which occurs quite frequently on some social media sites.<ref name="faculty.ist.psu.edu">{{cite journal | last1 = Jansen | first1 = B. J. | last2 = Zhang | first2 = M | last3 = Sobel | first3 = K | last4 = Chowdury | first4 = A | year = 2009 | title = Twitter Power: Tweets as Electronic Word of Mouth | url = https://faculty.ist.psu.edu/jjansen/academic/jansen_twitter_electronic_word_of_mouth.pdf | journal = Journal of the American Society for Information Science and Technology | volume = 60 | issue = 11 | pages = 2169–2188 | doi = 10.1002/asi.21149 | bibcode = 2009JASIS..60.2169J | s2cid = 260481339 | access-date = 2015-10-22 | archive-date = 2016-03-05 | archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20160305013618/https://faculty.ist.psu.edu/jjansen/academic/jansen_twitter_electronic_word_of_mouth.pdf | url-status = live }}</ref> Users can extend the reach of messages by sharing them with their connections and bringing more traffic to products/companies through [[word of mouth]].<ref name="Assaad" /> Businesses can interact with users directly and deliver targeted content based on user preferences. As users choose whom to follow on these sites, products can reach a very narrow target audience.<ref name="Assaad" />

Social networking sites also host information about what products and services prospective clients might be interested in. Marketers can use [[Semantics|semantic]] analysis technologies to detect buying signals, such as content shared by people and questions posted online. An understanding of buying signals can help [[salesperson|sales people]] target relevant prospects and marketers run [[microtargeting|micro-targeted]] campaigns.

In 2014, over 80% of business executives identified social media as an integral part of their business.<ref>{{Cite news|url=http://www.adweek.com/socialtimes/social-business-trends-2014/498129|title=Social Media Business Statistics, Facts, Figures & Trends 2014|last=Bennett|first=Shea|date=April 25, 2014|access-date=2016-04-06|archive-date=2016-04-06|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160406224150/http://www.adweek.com/socialtimes/social-business-trends-2014/498129|url-status=live}}</ref> Business retailers have seen 133% increases in their revenues from social media marketing.<ref>Bowden, J. (March 17, 2014). [http://www.socialmediatoday.com/content/impact-social-media-marketing-trends-digital-marketing The Impact of Social Media Marketing Trends on Digital Marketing] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141216212959/http://www.socialmediatoday.com/content/impact-social-media-marketing-trends-digital-marketing |date=2014-12-16 }}. socialmediatoday.com</ref>

==== Facebook ====
{{see also|Criticism of Facebook#Advertising|Pivot to video#Facebook metrics controversy}}
[[Facebook]] pages are more detailed than Twitter accounts. They allow a product to provide videos, photos, longer descriptions, and [[testimonials]] where followers can comment on the product pages for others to see. Facebook can link back to the product's Twitter page, as well as send out event reminders. As of May 2015, 93% of businesses marketers used Facebook to promote their brand.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.statista.com/statistics/259379/social-media-platforms-used-by-marketers-worldwide/|title=Social media platforms used by marketers worldwide 2015|access-date=6 October 2015|archive-date=7 October 2015|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20151007053106/http://www.statista.com/statistics/259379/social-media-platforms-used-by-marketers-worldwide/|url-status=live}}</ref>{{unreliable source?|date=October 2024|reason=See WP:STATISTA}}

A study from 2011 attributed 84% of "engagement" or clicks and likes that link back to Facebook advertising.<ref>{{cite web |url=http://www.mediapost.com/publications/article/160225/marketers-spend-more-on-mobile-search.html |title=Marketers Spend More |publisher=Mediapost.com |access-date=2011-12-21 |archive-date=2011-12-13 |archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20111213173726/http://www.mediapost.com/publications/article/160225/marketers-spend-more-on-mobile-search.html |url-status=live }}</ref> By 2014, Facebook had restricted the [[Content marketing|content]] published from business and brands. Adjustments in Facebook algorithms had reduced the audience for non-paying business pages (that have at least 500,000 likes) from 16% in 2012 down to 2% in February 2014.<ref>{{cite web|url = http://social.ogilvy.com/facebook-zero-considering-life-after-the-demise-of-organic-reach/|title = Facebook Zero: Considering Life After the Demise of Organic Reach|publisher = [[Ogilvy & Mather]]/Social@Ogilvy|last = Manson|first = Marshall|date = March 6, 2014|access-date = April 17, 2014|archive-date = April 19, 2014|archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20140419083149/http://social.ogilvy.com/facebook-zero-considering-life-after-the-demise-of-organic-reach|url-status = live}}</ref><ref>{{cite magazine|url = http://www.slate.com/blogs/future_tense/2014/03/24/facebook_brands_and_organic_reach_why_no_one_likes_your_self_promoting_posts.html|title = Facebook's Like Affair With Brands Is Over|magazine = [[Slate (magazine)|Slate]]|last = Oremus|first = Will|date = March 24, 2014|access-date = April 17, 2014|archive-date = April 16, 2014|archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20140416035301/http://www.slate.com/blogs/future_tense/2014/03/24/facebook_brands_and_organic_reach_why_no_one_likes_your_self_promoting_posts.html|url-status = live}}</ref><ref>{{cite magazine|url = http://digiday.com/platforms/facebook-agencies/|title = Facebook is not making friends on Madison Avenue|magazine = [[Digiday]]|last = McDermott|first = John|date = February 28, 2014|access-date = April 17, 2014|archive-date = April 19, 2014|archive-url = https://web.archive.org/web/20140419013500/http://digiday.com/platforms/facebook-agencies/|url-status = live}}</ref>

==== Instagram ====
{{main|Instagram#Advertising}}

[[File:Instagram app on smartphone (grass background) (cropped).jpg|thumb|The Instagram app]]
In May 2014, [[Instagram]] had over 200 million users. The user engagement rate of Instagram was 15 times higher than of Facebook and 25 times higher than that of Twitter.<ref>{{cite web|last=Michael|first=Estrin|title=10 huge marketing wins on Instagram|url=http://www.imediaconnection.com/content/36444.asp#ISpA0xhFUu2p2vUi.99|work=imediaconnection.com|access-date=2014-06-11|archive-date=2016-02-04|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160204180417/http://www.imediaconnection.com/content/36444.asp#ISpA0xhFUu2p2vUi.99|url-status=live}}</ref>

==== LinkedIn ====
Companies can create professional [[LinkedIn]] profiles for themselves and their business to network and meet others.<ref>{{cite web|title=About Us|url=http://press.linkedin.com/about|publisher=LinkedIn Press Center|access-date=28 March 2012|archive-date=9 February 2009|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20090209010700/http://press.linkedin.com/about|url-status=live}}</ref> LinkedIn members can use "Company Pages" similar to Facebook pages to create an area on which business owners can promote their products or services and interact with their customers.<ref>{{cite news|last=Slutsky|first=Irina|author-link=Irina Slutsky|title=Why LinkedIn Is the Social Network That Will Never Die|url=http://adage.com/article/digital/linkedin-social-network-die/147475/|access-date=29 March 2012|newspaper=AdAge Digital|date=29 March 2012|archive-date=11 April 2015|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150411093214/http://adage.com/article/digital/linkedin-social-network-die/147475/|url-status=live}}</ref>

==== Snapchat ====
{{main|Snapchat}}

Snapchat is an American multimedia instant messaging app. One of the principal features of Snapchat is that pictures and messages are usually available for only a short time before they become inaccessible to their recipients.

==== TikTok ====
{{main|TikTok}}

[[TikTok]] was first released in 2016 and became one of the most popular social media apps with over 1 billion users worldwide. It is mainly mobile-based and allows users to post short video content.{{citation needed|date=October 2024|reason=Source should be reliable, independent, and specifically explain how TikTok relates to social media marketing as an encyclopedia topic}}

==== Tumblr ====
Blogging website [[Tumblr]] first launched ad products on May 29, 2012.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://marketr.tumblr.com/post/54430724259/lets-kick-things-off-with-a-look-back-at-how-far|title=Marketr — Let's kick things off with a look back at how far...|access-date=12 September 2015|archive-date=9 September 2015|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150909204902/http://marketr.tumblr.com/post/54430724259/lets-kick-things-off-with-a-look-back-at-how-far|url-status=live}}</ref> Rather than relying on simple [[banner ad]]s, Tumblr requires advertisers to create a Tumblr blog so the content of those blogs can be featured on the site.<ref>{{cite web|url=http://www.technologyreview.com/view/514156/how-tumblr-forces-advertisers-to-get-creative/|title=How Tumblr Forces Advertisers to Get Creative|work=MIT Technology Review|date=24 April 2013 |access-date=12 September 2015|archive-date=7 September 2015|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150907103040/http://www.technologyreview.com/view/514156/how-tumblr-forces-advertisers-to-get-creative/|url-status=live}}</ref> In one year, four native ad formats were created on web and mobile, and had more than 100 brands advertising on Tumblr with 500 cumulative [[sponsored post]]s.

==== Twitter/X ====
{{see also|Twitter under Elon Musk|Sleeping Giants}}
[[Twitter]] allows companies to promote their products in short messages known as tweets limited to 280 characters which appear on followers' Home timelines.<ref>{{cite web|title=About your Twitter timeline|url=https://support.twitter.com/articles/164083|website=Twitter|access-date=29 April 2016|archive-date=24 April 2016|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160424215219/https://support.twitter.com/articles/164083|url-status=live}}</ref> Twitter has also been used by companies to provide customer service.<ref>{{cite magazine|last1=Holmes|first1=Ryan|title=Customer Service as a Spectator Sport Is About to End|url=https://time.com/3916355/social-media-customer-service/|access-date=7 July 2015|magazine=Time|archive-date=10 July 2015|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20150710033044/http://time.com/3916355/social-media-customer-service/|url-status=live}}</ref>

====Yelp====
[[Yelp, Inc.|Yelp]] consists of a comprehensive online index of business profiles. Businesses are searchable by location, similar to [[Yellow Pages]]. The website is operational in seven different countries, including the [[United States]] and [[Canada]]. Business account holders can create, share, and edit business profiles, and post information such as their business's location and contact information, along with pictures and service information. Individuals can write, post reviews about businesses, and rate them on a five-point scale. Messaging and talk features are additionally made available for general members of the website, serving to guide thoughts and opinions.<ref>{{cite web|last=Yelp|title=Faq|url=http://www.yelp.ca/faq#what_is_yelp|publisher=Faq|access-date=29 March 2012|archive-date=25 March 2012|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120325112304/http://www.yelp.ca/faq#what_is_yelp|url-status=live}}</ref>

====YouTube====
{{main|YouTube}}

Advertisements on [[YouTube]] can use [[targeted advertising]] via [[Google Ads]].

Advertisers can also sponsor videos directly, which is a form of [[native advertising]].<ref name="kincy">{{cite journal|last=Kincy|first=Jason|title=Advertising and social media|journal=ABA Bank Marketing|year=2011|volume=43|issue=7|page=40}}</ref> YouTube also enable publishers to earn money through its [[YouTube Partner Program]]. Companies can pay YouTube for a special "channel" which promotes the companies products or services.

===Social bookmarking sites===
[[Social bookmarking]] sites are used in social media promotion. Each of these sites is dedicated to the collection, [[Content curation|curation]], and organization of links to other websites that users deem to be of good quality. This process is "[[crowdsourced]]", allowing amateur social media network members to sort and prioritize links by relevance and general category. Due to the large user bases of these websites, any link from one of them to another, the smaller website may in a [[flash crowd]], a sudden surge of interest in the target website.

In addition to user-generated promotion, these sites also offer advertisements within individual user communities and categories.<ref>Lunden, Ingrid (2014-01-11) [https://techcrunch.com/2014/01/11/delicious-api-advertising/ Ad 'Experiments' Come To Delicious As It Updates Social Bookmarking API With Authentication, Rate Limits] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20170704214015/https://techcrunch.com/2014/01/11/delicious-api-advertising/ |date=2017-07-04 }}. techcrunch.com</ref> Because ads can be placed in designated communities with a very specific target audience and demographic, they have far greater potential for traffic generation than ads selected simply through [[HTTP cookie|cookie]] and browser history.<ref>Aaron, Jesse (2014-06-18) [https://www.theguardian.com/media-network/media-network-blog/2014/jun/18/integrate-reddit-digital-marketing "How to Integrate Reddit Into Your Next Digital Marketing Campaign"] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160325101224/http://www.theguardian.com/media-network/media-network-blog/2014/jun/18/integrate-reddit-digital-marketing |date=2016-03-25 }}. ''The Guardian''</ref>

Additionally, some of these websites have also implemented measures to make ads more relevant to users by allowing users to vote on which ones will be shown on pages they frequent.<ref>Parr, Benn (2009-08-06) [http://mashable.com/2009/08/06/digg-ads-2 "Digg Ads Are Here: Will Users Bury Them Into Oblivion?"] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141102092453/http://mashable.com/2009/08/06/digg-ads-2/ |date=2014-11-02 }} mashable.com</ref> The ability to redirect large volumes of [[web traffic]] and target specific, relevant audiences makes [[social bookmarking]] sites a valuable asset for social media marketers.

==Implications on traditional advertising==
===Minimizing use===
Traditional advertising techniques include print and [[television]] advertising. The [[Internet]] has already overtaken television as the largest advertising market.<ref>[https://www.theguardian.com/media/2009/sep/30/internet-biggest-uk-advertising-sector Internet overtakes television to become biggest advertising sector in the UK] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20161201223148/https://www.theguardian.com/media/2009/sep/30/internet-biggest-uk-advertising-sector |date=2016-12-01 }}. ''The Guardian'' (2009-09-30)</ref>
[[Websites|Web sites]] often include [[banner ad|banner]] or [[pop-up ad]]s. Social networking sites do not always carry ads. In exchange, products have entire pages and are able to interact with users. [[Television commercials]] often end with a [[spokesperson]] asking viewers to check out the product website for more information. While briefly popular, print ads included QR codes which can be scanned by [[cell phone]]s and [[computers]], sending viewers to the product website. Advertising is beginning to move viewers from traditional outlets to electronic ones.<ref>{{Cite web | url=https://www.business.com/articles/how-technology-is-changing-online-advertising/ | title=How Technology is Changing Online Advertising | access-date=2019-11-01 | archive-date=2019-11-01 | archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20191101085641/https://www.business.com/articles/how-technology-is-changing-online-advertising/ | url-status=live }}</ref>

===Mishaps===
Due to the viral nature of the Internet, mishaps with social media posts can be damaging for organizations.

In 2011, designer [[Kenneth Cole (designer)|Kenneth Cole]] tweeted, "Millions are in uproar in #Cairo. Rumor has they heard our new spring collection is now available online at [Kenneth Cole's website]".<ref name="ibtimes">[http://www.ibtimes.com/articles/108811/20110204/kenneth-cole-tweets-egypt-cairo-politically-incorrect-inappropriate-kc-pr-twitter-facebook-apology-h.htm Twitter account dedicated to poke fun at Kenneth Cole for #Cairo tweet] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120618092907/http://www.ibtimes.com/articles/108811/20110204/kenneth-cole-tweets-egypt-cairo-politically-incorrect-inappropriate-kc-pr-twitter-facebook-apology-h.htm |date=2012-06-18 }}. Ibtimes.com (2011-02-04). Retrieved on 2013-01-11.</ref> This reference to the [[2011 Egyptian revolution]] drew an objection from the public; it was widely objected to on the Internet.<ref name="ibtimes" /> Kenneth Cole realized his mistake shortly after and responded with a statement apologizing for the tweet.<ref>[https://www.adweek.com/performance-marketing/kenneth-coles-twitter-fail/ Kenneth Cole's Twitter Fail – PRNewser] . Mediabistro.com (2011-02-03). Retrieved on 2013-01-11.</ref>

In 2011, a [[Chrysler Group]] employee tweeted that no one in Detroit knows how to drive.<ref name="High price to be paid for controversial social-media mishaps">[http://www.sfgate.com/cgi-bin/article.cgi?f=/c/a/2011/03/16/BUA61ID19P.DTL High price to be paid for controversial social-media mishaps] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20110320193528/http://www.sfgate.com/cgi-bin/article.cgi?f=%2Fc%2Fa%2F2011%2F03%2F16%2FBUA61ID19P.DTL |date=2011-03-20 }} ''[[San Francisco Chronicle]]'' March 17, 2011, retrieved April 4, 2012</ref>

In 2012, during [[Hurricane Sandy]], [[Gap Inc.|Gap]] sent out a tweet to its followers telling them to stay safe, but encouraged them to shop online and offered free shipping. The tweet was deemed insensitive, and Gap eventually took it down and apologized.<ref>Wasserman, T. (2012-10-31). [http://mashable.com/2012/10/31/gap-tweet-hurricane-sandy/ Gap Criticized For Insensitive Tweet During Hurricane Sandy] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141124225846/http://mashable.com/2012/10/31/gap-tweet-hurricane-sandy/ |date=2014-11-24 }}. mashable.com</ref>

When [[the Link REIT]] opened a Facebook page to recommend old-style restaurants, the page was flooded by furious comments criticizing the REIT for having forced a lot of restaurants and stores to shut down; it had to terminate its campaign early amid further deterioration of its corporate image.<ref>[http://lifestyle.etnet.com.hk/column/index.php/management/executive/10010?locdes=content 領匯「尋味」腰斬 如何拆網絡炸彈 (The Link terminates its search for "old tastes": How to improve online corporate image] {{Webarchive|url=https://web.archive.org/web/20120423122611/http://lifestyle.etnet.com.hk/column/index.php/management/executive/10010?locdes=content |date=2012-04-23 }}{{in lang|zh}}, ''[[Hong Kong Economic Times]]'' April 20, 2012, retrieved April 25, 2012</ref>

In 2018, [[Max Factor]], [[MAC Cosmetics|MAC]] and other beauty brands were forced to rush to disassociate themselves from Kuwaiti beauty blogger and Instagram 'influencer' [[Sondos Alqattan]] after she criticised government moves to improve conditions for domestic workers.<ref>{{Cite news|url=https://www.thenationalnews.com/lifestyle/mac-cosmetics-releases-statement-denouncing-kuwaiti-influencer-sondos-al-qattan-1.753973|title=MAC Cosmetics releases statement denouncing Kuwaiti influencer Sondos Al Qattan|work=The National|access-date=2018-07-26|language=en|archive-date=2018-07-25|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20180725185654/https://www.thenational.ae/lifestyle/mac-cosmetics-releases-statement-denouncing-kuwaiti-influencer-sondos-al-qattan-1.753973|url-status=live}}</ref>

===Ethics===
The ethical principles associated with traditional marketing can also be applied to social media.<ref>{{Cite journal|last1=Yao|first1=Becatien|last2=Shanoyan|first2=Aleksan|last3=Peterson|first3=Hikaru Hanawa|last4=Boyer|first4=Cheryl|last5=Baker|first5=Lauri|date=April 2019|title=The use of new-media marketing in the green industry: Analysis of social media use and impact on sales|journal=Agribusiness|language=en|volume=35|issue=2|pages=281–297|doi=10.1002/agr.21581|s2cid=158549706|issn=0742-4477}}</ref> However, with social media being so personal and international, online ethics come with additional complications and challenges. A sensitive topic amongst social media professionals is the subject of ethics in social media marketing practices, specifically the proper uses of, often, very personal data.<ref>{{Cite journal|last=Ford|first=John B.|date=December 2019|title=What Do We Know About Social-Media Marketing?|journal=Journal of Advertising Research|language=en|volume=59|issue=4|pages=383–384|doi=10.2501/JAR-2019-041|issn=0021-8499|doi-access=free}}</ref> With social media, marketers can see what consumers like to hear from advertisers, how they engage online, and what their needs and wants are, instead of focusing solely on the basic demographics and [[psychographics]] given from television and magazines.<ref>{{cite web|last1=Gutierrez|first1=Mateo|title=The Ethics of Social Media Marketing|url=http://www.huffingtonpost.com/mateo-gutierrez/social-media-marketing_b_2206357.html|website=Huffington Post|access-date=19 November 2014|date=2012-12-03|archive-date=2014-12-07|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20141207172419/http://www.huffingtonpost.com/mateo-gutierrez/social-media-marketing_b_2206357.html|url-status=live}}</ref>

The general concept of ethical social media usage entails [[honesty]] with a campaign's intentions, avoiding false advertising, awareness of user [[privacy]] conditions (which means not using consumers' private information for gain), respecting people's dignity, and taking responsibility for mistakes or mishaps that result from marketing campaigns.<ref>{{cite book|last1=Neher|first1=Melissa S. Barker, Donald I. Barker, Nicholas F. Bormann, Krista E.|title=Social media marketing: a strategic approach|date=2013|publisher=South-Western Cengage Learning|location=Mason, OH|isbn=978-0-538-48087-1|pages=76–78|edition=1st|url=https://books.google.com/books?id=kdgKAAAAQBAJ&q=ethics+of+social+media+marketing&pg=PT93|access-date=28 November 2014|archive-date=14 August 2022|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20220814100025/https://books.google.com/books?id=kdgKAAAAQBAJ&q=ethics+of+social+media+marketing&pg=PT93|url-status=live}}</ref> Most social network marketers use websites like Facebook and [[MySpace]] to try to drive traffic to another website.<ref>{{cite web|url=https://aipmm.com/html/newsletter/archives/000270.php|title=Ethics is Social Network Marketing|last1=Skul|first1=David|website=AIPMM|date=9 April 2008|url-status=live|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20140812231814/https://aipmm.com/html/newsletter/archives/000270.php|archive-date=2014-08-12|access-date=29 November 2014}}</ref>

In addition, social media platforms have become extremely aware of their users and collect information about their viewers to connect with them in various ways. Facebook is quietly working on a new advertising system that would let marketers target users with ads based on the massive amounts of information people reveal on the site about themselves.<ref>{{cite journal |author1=Saravanakumar, M. |author2=SuganthaLakshmi, T. |name-list-style=amp |year=2012 |title=Social media marketing |url=https://www.lifesciencesite.com/lsj/life0904/670_13061life0904_4444_4451.pdf |journal=Life Science Journal |volume=9 |pages=4444–4451}}</ref>

Some people may react negatively because they believe it is an invasion of privacy. On the other hand, some individuals may enjoy this feature because their social network recognizes their interests and sends them particular advertisements pertaining to those interests. Consumers like to network with people who share their interests and desires.<ref>{{cite journal|author1=Mangold, W.G. |author2=Faulds, D.J. |name-list-style=amp |year=2009|title =Social media: The new hybrid element of the promotion mix|journal=Business Horizons |volume= 52|issue=4 |pages= 357–365|doi=10.1016/j.bushor.2009.03.002}}</ref> Managers invest in social media to foster relationships and interact with customers.<ref>{{cite journal|author=de Vries, L., Gensler, S. and Leeflang, P. |year=2012|title=Popularity of brand posts on brand fan pages: An investigation of the effects of social media marketing|journal= Journal of Interactive Marketing|volume= 26|issue=2|pages= 83–91|doi=10.1016/j.intmar.2012.01.003|s2cid=168073699 |url=https://research.rug.nl/en/publications/4f135800-2e1d-4d28-a9d3-5e09cc475890 |hdl=11370/4f135800-2e1d-4d28-a9d3-5e09cc475890|hdl-access=free}}</ref>

For many users, data collection is a breach of privacy, but there are no laws that prevent these companies from using the information provided on their websites. Companies like [[Equifax]], [[TransUnion]], and [[LexisNexis]] thrive on collecting and sharing the personal information of social media users.<ref name="jitpl.jmls.edu">{{cite web|last1=Hochheiser|first1=Megan|title=Summary Of Data Mining Issues and Regulations|url=http://jitpl.jmls.edu/2015/04/02/summary-of-data-mining-issues-and-regulations/#_ftnref3|website=The John Marshall Journal of Information Technology and Privacy Law|access-date=April 17, 2016|archive-date=April 20, 2016|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20160420054027/http://jitpl.jmls.edu/2015/04/02/summary-of-data-mining-issues-and-regulations/#_ftnref3|url-status=live}}</ref> In 2012, Facebook purchased information about 70 million households from a third-party company called [[Datalogix]]. Facebook later revealed that they purchased the information in order to create a more efficient advertising service.<ref>{{cite web|last1=Broderick|first1=Ryan|last2=Grinberg|first2=Emanuella|title=10 ways you give up data without knowing it|url=http://www.cnn.com/2013/06/13/living/buzzfeed-data-mining/|website=CNN|date=13 June 2013|access-date=18 April 2017|archive-date=18 April 2017|archive-url=https://web.archive.org/web/20170418164412/http://www.cnn.com/2013/06/13/living/buzzfeed-data-mining/|url-status=live}}</ref>
